» keen indignation

Two Observations 


T found out mich, but before

get 


One big item which can be pub-

Germans, 


Saved For The Day 


‘They are being saved” for the 
day- when Hitler attenipts to make 
good his boast that he wil) fill the 
air with aeroplanes, If ever he 
does that, his losses will be ter- 
rific. Meanwhile, these units re- 
main carefully camouflaged and 
the guns are not discharged be- 
cause the British do not wish to 
betray ‘their locations to the 
enemy.

was explained to me: & 


“We have set oul trap; ahd it's 
a big trap, and we are waiting to 
catch something big’ in it. Until 
ithe crisis comes, we are guarding

In the meantime, we use other 
defence units as fully as possible. 
Also, in the meantime, German 
‘planes are getting through to our 
cities and deing damage. But we

are saving the big stuff for the

Warriors At Ease 


“NOT long ago we mentioned 

“©The Third Business Man” on 
this page. We referred to him in 
the past tense, but objections have 
been loud and numerous. The 
argument is that one's brains, hav- 


ing,been exercised by day in the | 


office, need relaxation in the even- 
ing, and that any- attempt toover- 
load. ‘the méfital. processes is 
bound -to résult in early disinte- 
gration. 7 
_RB nt om 

I" is refreshing, therefore, to find 

;, at least one set of people who 
think otherwise. This is the 
R.A.0.C. Warrant Officers and 
Sergeants Dining Club. ‘There 
are, in HongKong as elsewhere, 
a number of luncheon jelubs that 
serve a similar purpese, but, as 
ar as) we:can discover, the 
R.4.0.C. club is the only one that 
invites interesting and distinguish- 
ed people to discourse to them in 
the'evening. And more than that, 
in spite of a large dinner and 
some- liquid refreshment, they 
actudlly do their speakers the 
honour of remaining awake to 
Jisten to them. 


2 & Lad 

HOW much the visiting speakers 

appreciate this may be judged 
from the fact that they ,generally 
ask, to-be allowed to return in the 
role of ordinary. guests, Their 
last two -speakers were the Hon 
NL. Smith and Mr. K. M. Av 
Barnett. The latter may be des- 
cribed as the’ club’s "unofficial 
patron, Since its inception 4 
year ago; he has missed only one 
dinner, and his entertaining talk 
last Friday on “Chinese Marriage 
Customs” was the second he has 
given there. 
: ee + 
JHE credit for the enterprise Be- 

longs to several members, and 
fo the encouragement of the 
officers of the R.A.O.C., but spe- 
tial mention must be made of 
Sergeant Mitchell, ‘the secretary. 
He does most of.the hard wotk, 
the -arranging of the dinners, the 
designing of the’ menus and 
“dating” of the speakers, In ad- 
dition he adds a’pleasant touch, of 
his own, ‘in, the shape of the apt 
and sometimes pungent, quota~ 
tions, which ate appended ‘to each” 
of the items ‘oh ‘the meni: card.

menu-cards, find their.way to Aus- 
tralta,. but we prefer to Keep ours

F there is anythitg in the view 
popularly held in some quar- 
ters that the Chinese hold on life 
is peculiar to itself, that’ death 
may revult from the slightest in- 
cident, or, on the contrary,’-that 
he may survive. an_ injury suf- 
ficient to kill any other man twice 
over, it would seem to be confirm- 
ed by an incident at the Queen 
Mary recently.: 


g 7 & : 
FTER walking about with a 
sore on the head for a fort- 
night, a. man went. into - hospita) 
to see if they could-do something 
to make it heal up. No-one in. the 
hospital believed it’ to be ‘other 
than a joke when the X-ray plate 
showed a nail two . and: a’ half 
inches long which had _ entered 
the skull straight down from the 
top of the head and-penetratéd the 
brain, No sign of thé nail was. 
visible on the surface, but the 
“obstruction” : was removed © by 
“operation and. the man is out dnd 
about to-dey ag though nothing 
had happened. : 


HE told -amazed medical mien 

that he had: been stru¢k over 
the head during a street fight, ‘and 
Was temporarily knocked out,. and 
that all he knew about the nail 
was that the 
‘heal! Which, perhaps, was just. as 
well, The mere thouglit of two 
and a half inches 6f nail in the 
. brain would be enough to doom 
many people. |

What Women Can. Do , 


Women? Does the thought of 
allthis leave you with that queer 
feeling in the. pit? Don't. worry. 

Twelve women took shelter in 
a crypt. Fire Bombs dropped ail 
around. ‘They came out to fetch 
and .carry sand and water.. Saw 
how the warden methodically 
tathed the bombs. Got a twelve 
-foot ladder. This party of women 
alone put out seven bombs in ten 
minutes. 


Not far away a girl of 15, who 
had never, beeh In a raid-before, 
put out four bombs in an office 
where her father is caretaker, He 
was busy In the next building. . 

Once you have put out the first 
bomb and seen how easily it can 
be done, you're keen on collecting 
a few more scalps. 

Now for tips for. different types 
of building. 

Houses first:. Easiest of atl, For 
if a couple fall through your roof 
you’ve had a very full ration: 

Bomb generally—bounces—. off 
roof or lodges in altic. But make 
sure garage roof isn’t the flat type 
with felt overboards for covering. 
A. bomb falling there will svon 
start a blaze. Have a quick look- 
see as soon as stuff rains down. 

Don’t stay in the dugout and 
expect others to do your work for 
you, They can’t see what is hap- 
pening in your attic. Either be 
on the spot or arrange for imme- 
ate warhing when flre-bombs 
‘atl. 

Then up to the top storey. 1 
hope you've ‘got it. cleared. Got 
buckets of dry sand: ready? That 
stirrap pump? Reserve of water? 
Good, 

Let the stirrup party get to 
work if it is convenient, Other- 
wise use sand and rake, quenching 
the fire where the bomb has first 
come to rest, : 

Perhaps you've atrived ay,bit 


late._The bomb is eating througir 


the ‘plaster work into the room. 


below. 

Send someone into, that’ room. 
They will see the spot where the 
metal is trickling through. The 
ceiling at.that point will first go 
brown or black. 

Make sure tnere is nothing in- 
flammable where it will fall 
through. Shift the bed if below 
the spot. The curtains if they 
are toa near. Stand a bucket of 
sand under the spot, Then you'll 
be safe. 

All this time the fire-fighter in 
the loft is chipping around with 
that useful hatchet, and raking 
out as much of the bomb as. he 
can. 

Perhaps you'll be so slow that 
the house is alight good and 
hearty. Evacuation will be your 
fate, Make sure everything you 
treasure is ready packed on the 
ground floor, Amazing what. you 
can get away in a couple of min- 
utes if it’s all handy. ,

The Best Position 


“3 : 

Here’s 4 point that worries many 
. people, * eee 
Where to thke up position? 

On the roof, or on the top floor? 

Old-timers swear by. the main 
door. . 

Keen it open, stand behind it, 
and, if there’s no glass, you're 
quite safe when. the HE. ‘falls. 

You're in the correct. position ta 
hear the short and sharp whistie 
of the little incendiary followed 
by a little plop-or by: silence. 

Chief .trouble in offices ts the 
-small, cluttered-up room. Heat, 
dazzle, spiuttering metal can—be 
rather terrifying then. e 

Open the door -cautiously. Keep 
it between the.bomb! and the body 
until the bomb js‘ located, 

To save “dazzle-blindness”. 
wear 2 peaked cap, and, with head 
down, look at the blinding area 
only through .the top of the eye. 
This will enable the exact loca- 
tion of thé bomb to be fixed and 
the sand to be accurately thrown. 

Many old-timers wear smoked 
gogeles for this. Others in addition 
have a “face-shield” of rica. This 
4s pulled’ down into position when 
the. bomb must be approached 
very closely, und saves the eyes 
from jumpin beads of metal. 

This and: the goggles cah be 

fixed, one at the back, one at the 
front of a tin hat) and quickly 
turned into position. 
. Other equipment, consists of a 
boiler suit and - wellingtotis—the 
latter ‘to save the feet from burn- 
ing, and in case of flooding from 
the fire brigade. 

Other veterans carry a 40 -foot 
length of rope when on tricky 
roofs. Help, to haul themselves 
in nasty positions while tackling 
bombs. —~ 
~ Should evacuation of the build- 
ing be necessary, then typewriters, 
duplicators, &c., should be ept 
handy on the ground ftoor. They 
can be got away easily. Never" pu! 
them in a basement, Everyth'ne 
there.is quickly ruined, largely be- 
_calise water enters there naturally

Weak spots mapped out, Leave

mable material under these weak }- 
spots. ae: 

Keep sand and water at strate- 
gic points all over the roof. Cover

with buckets of sand; finally. the 
gvater.

order is always-kept right.

sion of $94,000,000 credit loans for 
‘the ‘development of Kwangst 
agriculture has ‘been concluded 
between the Kwangsi Provincial 
Government, and the four ‘Gov- 
ernment Banks and the Central 
Trust. 


Some $12,000,000 will be for 
general agricultural projects, 
$10,000,000 for farmers in the. war 
zones, $10, 000,000 for water con- 
gervancy and irrigation projects

Ingredients: 1 lb. four, pinch, of 
salt, 1 teaspoonful Jemon juice, 
cold water, 12 ozs, butter (or but- 
ler and lard mixed). : 

Pass the flour and salt.through a° 

wire sieve into a bowl, Cut up. the 
butter (or butter and lard) into 
pieces about the size of a walnut, 
drop it into. the flour, add. the. 
water and lemon juice mixed; and 
form quickly into a-soft paste. . 
- Turn out on to the board and 
roll into a strip. Sprinkle lightly 
with flour, fold in three, press the 
edges together, give the pastry a 
half turn to the right and roll 
again. 

Repeat the folding, turning and 
rolling until the fat is thoroughly 
mixed into the pastry, but leave 
the pastry to.gtand.in a.coo]l place 
between every other rolling ” if 
possible. 7 

It is also improved by standing 
ina cool place: for an: hour -or: tivo 
before using. " :

This week-dnd ‘had, onée 
again, the thrilling oeneies of 
welcoming and assisting survivors 
of a cargo ship who réached our 
island “in a lifeboat, Their tale. 
although quite an ordinary one to 
them, seems to me_ dramatic 
benough to repeat here in brief. 

“It was 700 miles out in the 
Atlantic. The cargo ship has 
developed engine trouble and falls 
out of the convoy. ‘There is _ an 
explosion amidships. Half a doz- 
én of the crew are killed: the 
rest of them, twenty-three in 
number, launch one of the ship's 
lifeboats and set a-course eas:- 
ward in the darkness. There is 
fno sign of the submarine which 
attacked them: Fortunately _one 
member of ‘the crew, an old. Irish 
! fisherman, can handle a sail. 
' Stormy days pass, day by day, 
into tempestuous night. One by 
one the mén die of exposure, After 
s@ven days in that waste of men- 
acing water not a drop of fresh 
water is left in the kegs for’ the 
crew. Eight men have died al- 
ready, ard three more die before 
the Senior Apprentice sights the 
dim outline - of land, — Almost 
immediately ‘afterwards the ‘mist 
closes down upon them. again, 
and the sea rises, About ten 
o'clock that night, after -eleven 
days of battling with cold andl 
savage weather the lifeboat ent 
the only cove of a small Heb: 
ean island in which it wou

Rifle S 


In Hong 


(By A Special 


Range. 


It is interesting, therefore, to re- 
call that before the present. asso~ 
clation was formed, rifle shoot- 
ing in Hong Kong had a very leon 
spell from.1928, when the old Rifle 
‘League was ‘wound up, until Ma~ 
jor..Steers,"R.E., managed to in- 
fuse enough interest amongst the 
tifle shots: in.'the’ Colony .to ‘form 
a new association, How, that body 
has grown. from strength to 
strength, “since its ‘inception in|

proficiency is to be attained. A, E. Carey, the Police 
veteran, is the exception that proves the rule. He 
played in his first match at the age of 30, took up 


‘bowling seven years later, 


ing with more success than ever before,.as evidence 
his fine bowling for Police against University last 
week, which gave last season's champions their first: 
points in five League matches. .. 9 |: 


“OE” Carey is a native of the 
cricketing county of Kent and | 
although he had always been keen j 
on, the game he | had no ‘oppor- 
“tunity of playing until he. joined j 
Hong Kong Police shortly after! 
the Great War, arriving here at; 
the age of 18, rs | 
Even then, although he. could | 
have played “ai Police ‘Club, he | 
Police work ‘was entirely new ta | 
hhim—he ‘had spent his earlier days | 
as an apprentice engincer—and | 
he was keen on absorbing all the | 
knowledge necessary. to a custo- + 
dian of the law in the quickest 
possible time. a : 
For the next 11 years. therefore, 
his only association with cricket 
was as a spectafor at Police Club. 
Police cricket in those days was 
“not very clever,” the team hav- 
ing a longer-tail than the present 
a but there were one, or two 
men who were reilly good, nat- | 
ably C, FL Alexander, who despite | 
the poor cluss of cricket in which

ferport trial, former Commission*

“Ginger” Baker,

IN POLICE XI! 
N 1936, Carey then nearly 30, 
wus still wrapped up in his; 
work and: devoted no time to re- 
creation, but his  dgctor took a 
hand in things and he was order- 
ed to take more exercise, At the 
first opportunity, therefore, Carey 
worked his way into the Police 
cricket team, more as a stop-gap 
than anything else, but his keen- 
ness secured him a regular place. 
His batting average at the end of | 
that season worked out at the: 
grand figure of 4! 
Constant practice, which by 
this time the doctor did not have 
to prescribe, saw vast improve- 


.geason.and his place in thr eleven 

thereafter. has never been in 
doubt, 

His best season was in 1837, 
when. he scored ovér 600 runs 
and easily headed the Police bat- 
ting averages, and he has always 
been to the fore since 1931.

MOST THRILLS 


ARKY considers the most excit- 
ing match he has ever played 
in was the play-off with T.R.C, for 
the ‘championship last season, 
Postooned twice’ on account of the 
weather, the match was: finally 
decided at Sookunpoo on May 12, 
a dul humid Sunday. 

There were numerous support- 
ers of both teams present, ‘despite 
the lateness of the season, and the 
game was followed with intense 
interest, Police batted first and 
made 131, Hunter scoring 31, Fay 
25, Pope 22 and Kirby 20, and 
LBC. were then dismissed for 126 
by Fay and Lewis, the closing 
stages of the match being really, 

” 


thrilling 
HISTORIC GAME 


AREY'S most memorable match 
was the now almost: historic, 
fixture between Police and Volun- | 
teers, which was played some 
years ago on a Sunday that coin-} 
cided with Arthur Beck’s birth-; 
day. Beck celebrated the occasion | 
by scoring a century, but the fun! 
really started when the game 
finished and both sides got down, 
to serious team-shaking! 

Carey cannot remember when 
the party finally broke up, but its 
conylviality can be judged by the 
fact that (at 8 p.m, ‘three large! 
baskets full of empty beer bottles! 
were seen leaving the clubhouse, 
and both teams were still intact! 

Alec Lowson at the piano pro- 
vided the music and all members 
ot both teams were required to 
sing, which they did with more; 
gusto than harmony, Included in’ 
the Volunteer team, besides Beck 
and Lowson, were. the late W. L.| 
MeKenzie, R,.H. Griffiths, N. A, E. 
Mackay and Bill Stoker, while 
Police were represented, among 
others,-by Carey, Hunter, Baker, 
Hon. Mr. T. H: King, Alexander, 
Loughlin, Forrest, Kirby and Dan- 
browsky. 

Fixtures against the Volunteers 
have always been. highlights of 
the Palice séason and the enforced 
cancellation of the ‘matches this 
year has been the cause of much 
Police regret, which is shared by 
the eight or so members of the 
Corps who had __ entered their 
names on the waiting-list!

QUE of the: odditles “of uman 

nature is the ‘readinkss to ac- 
cept a misconception. and the: per- 
sistence with which we.cling to it. 
| Offhand, we don't’ know of a 
‘single subject invested. with more 
cockeyed misbeliefe - than 
friend the dog, Without doubt, 
the Number One of.all misconcep- 


the person who doesn’t: like dogs, 
or vice versa, is one who'll bear 
watching. Nonsense.’ Many: fine’ 
people’ dqn’t like dogs; with: or 
without reason: «+. °° 7: : 
Another. fairy tale: concerns’ the 
dog that howls ‘at: night for: no 
apparent reason. In certain quar- 


sign of death,-whereas the reason 


id| of 


tor ‘this: vocal demonstration -of 
Fido's may be nothing more than. 
a desire to declare himself, or 
}even .plain, loneliness. If we pe- 
cept as more scientific: explana- 
tion, it’s to the effect that such 
salos are merely an out-cropping 
instinct. Our — four-legred. 
friend obeys a primitive impulse 


which comes down to him. from 
that distant time when all dogs 


id} hunted in packs—the howl being 
-l the rallying call tor the hunt. 


Stil! another fable devoutly be- 
lieved by some is that barking 
dogs seldom-bite. Don't-trust this 
one. Our milkman did — and 
then, as a concession to modesty, 
went home to ghange his pants. 

Many people believe tha’ mad 
dogs rush around wildly. This is 
only half ‘true. There are “two 
forms of hydrophobia: in one the 
dog: goes .on a rampage;'in the 
other he sulks in dark corners. 

When Fido. is moved to a fit, 
this may be caused either by ner - 
vous shock, digestive " disorder, 
worms, yes, even water starva- 
tion, or any of a number of sim~- 
ple reasons having nothing to do 
with rabies, Furthermore,-. the 
dog with a foaming mouth. suff-_ 
ers from no more than a harm- 
less fit, and his bite at that time 
is no more. dangerous than ‘any 
other wound that requires dress- 
ing and sterilisation,

Vonarares 


By Edward Faust. 


It’s time to debunk our 


friend, the bow-wow, and 


tell truth instead of fairy tales about him. © 


t 


entire breed for the failings of a 
few is as obviously unfair as to 
indict a nation for the idiocies of 
a few of.its people. 


For the dog that may be quick 
to resent over-familiar handling; 
there is some allowance. to ‘be 
made, To: begin with, most. of 
the breeds that have been thus 
maligned .are found - among the 
dogs of ‘the working group, such 
as the Collie, the Doberman Pins- 
cher, the German Shepherd, |, the 
Aretie breeds, Samoyede, Male~ 
mute, and -kindred dogs. Dogs 
of these breeds were primarily 
developed for work—to herd, to 
pull small.carts or .sleds and 
protect lives and, property. . 

The ancestors of these dogs 
spent their lives in isolated rural 
areas, where they saw few strang- 


.fers and were very likely taught 


to regard those they did see with 
suspicion, 

Now there’s one dog that’s 
slandered ‘more. than any other-— 
the Bloodhound. In the popular 
mind he's regarded as a four-leg- 
ged terror particularly fond of 
escaping _ criminals, Actually, 
he’s one of the most gentle of all. 
dogs. But possessing remarkable 
scenting power, he is astonishing- 


ly: successful on the trail. Hence, ; 


he*has become the choice of law 
enforcement officers in’ tracking 
eriminals—and lost persons, too. 
He's not a bit savage—on the trail 
or off. His only duties. are to 
track his quarry, and hold it at 
bay until John Law arrives. 
“But, you'll say, “look at the 
name. Doesn't that suggest blood- 
shed?” Well, it has to do with 
blood, but not in the way that's 
commonly believed, This dog was 
one of the first. whose breeding 
was carefully supervised to retain 
certain qualities, For this reason 
it beeame known as the blooded 
hound, just as to-day we refer 
blooded horses or blooded stock 
of any kind to indicate ‘the pure- 
bred. : 
Another dog that lives in. the 
shadows is the so-called French 
Poodle. To begin with, he’s no! 
at all French but is instead a 
German dog. ; His name is deriv- 
ord. pudel, mean- 
it fits him to a T, 
ft the finest of all 
water retrievers. Because the 
French were quick to popularise 
him, he wrongly 


the ‘French Poodie. He's not at

Our old friend the English Bull- 

dog is another whose real. worth 


the most ferocious ‘looking brutes 
alive, he is actually, a: gentle, pat- 
jent creature. .He’s an Eng! ish- 
man and. gets him name from the 
fact that long ago he was develop- 
ed for the alleged sport of bull- 
baiting. Because of his tolerant 
disposition, he’s ideal for children, 
—a real gentleman. . 


Doggone? 


Lost dogs can now be positive- 
ly identified by their noseprints, 
which are as accurate for dogs as 
fingerprints are for people, New 
York’s| Bide-A-Wee- Home for 
‘Animals has worked out'a system 
of taking and registering the 
prints and, through Noseprints, 
Inc., offers dog owners & service 
of finding strayed pets and prov~ 
ing their ownership for a - small 
registration fee. . ;

If people. would open their

ly, without worry, then everyone

himself unsuspected qualities. of 
intelligence, and would find that 
there is no need to fear the spon-
